---
name: optimization-modeling-core
description: Use for natural-language-to-optimization tasks that require translating a business problem into a complete mathematical model before writing solver code.
metadata:
  short-description: Turn business text into a full optimization model
---

# Optimization Modeling Core

## Use when

- The user gives a word problem, business process, paper scenario, or benchmark prompt and expects a formal optimization model.
- Solver code exists but the issue appears to be a missing or incorrect formulation.
- The task asks for a complete model, not only a heuristic, simulation, or data summary.

## Do not use when

- The task is purely about software plumbing, visualization, data cleaning, or report writing.
- The problem is already fully formulated and the user only asks for code translation into a specific solver; use the solver-specific skill as the primary guide.
- The request is exploratory and the user explicitly wants brainstorming without implementation.

## Model-first workflow

1. Restate the business decision in plain language.
2. Identify sets, indices, units, and time granularity.
3. Extract parameters from data files and confirm units.
4. Define decision variables with domains and business meaning.
5. Write the objective with every cost, reward, penalty, and service term.
6. Write constraints grouped by operational rule.
7. Check edge cases: missing eligibility, impossible demand, fixed commitments, and terminal-state requirements.
8. Only then implement code.

## Ambiguity handling

- If the brief is ambiguous but data and examples imply a convention, state the assumption and continue.
- If ambiguity changes feasibility, objective direction, or deliverable schema, ask before coding or implement the least risky interpretation with a clear note.
- If units conflict across files, normalize units explicitly before modeling.
- If a hard requirement conflicts with data, diagnose the conflict before adding slack variables.

## Formulation checklist

- Every row in an input table should either become a parameter, an eligibility rule, or be explicitly irrelevant.
- Every decision variable should appear in at least one binding constraint and usually in the objective.
- Demand balance should use the correct equality or inequality direction; do not weaken equality to `>=` unless surplus is genuinely allowed.
- Capacity constraints should include activation/open/setup binaries when the business says inactive resources cannot produce, ship, or serve.
- Shortage, backlog, overtime, outsourcing, and postponement variables need explicit cost and bounds.
- Multi-period models need state transition equations and initial/terminal states.
- If the user asks for explanation, describe the modeling logic in business terms after solving.

## Constraint boundary rules

- Hard constraints represent physical impossibility, policy mandates, legal requirements, or explicit benchmark feasibility rules.
- Soft constraints need penalty variables or objective terms; do not silently turn hard constraints into penalties.
- Service levels should specify numerator, denominator, eligible population, and time window.
- Fairness constraints should define the comparison group and whether unavailable resources count.
- Logical implications need both directions only when the business rule says equivalence, not merely implication.

## Objective boundary rules

- Confirm whether the problem minimizes cost, maximizes profit, maximizes service, or uses lexicographic priorities.
- Do not mix penalties with primary economics unless their scale is intentional.
- Include fixed costs only once per activated entity.
- Include revenue only for fulfilled demand or accepted jobs, not for unmet or canceled work.
- Keep reporting metrics separate from optimized objective terms unless the prompt explicitly optimizes them.

## Implementation expectations

- Use structured parsing for CSV/JSON instead of manual string manipulation.
- Keep solver code, solution export, and validation code clearly separated.
- For benchmark or task environments, read the provided brief before coding and follow the expected output schema exactly.
- When comparing two answers, diagnose concrete formulation differences and objective consequences, not only feasibility.
- Write exported solutions at the precision expected by the checker; avoid unnecessary rounding.
- Preserve the input schema and output schema unless the user asks to redesign the benchmark.

## Verification boundary

- Verify feasibility independently from solver status when an evaluator or checker exists.
- Recompute objective from exported artifacts, not only from in-memory solver expressions.
- For benchmark tasks, create at least one negative check for each newly added rule when practical.
- For paper or real-world tasks, separate what is directly supported by the source from synthetic or assumed data.

## Red flags

- Objective terms that are counted twice or charged at the wrong marginal rate.
- Binary variables not linked tightly enough to continuous activity.
- Time periods shifted by one index.
- Average or fairness constraints that ignore the denominator or eligibility set.
- Public prompt files that reveal solver-ready variables or formulas when the benchmark is meant to test modeling translation.
- A feasible solution that exploits missing eligibility, free disposal, negative cycles, or unbounded production.
- A model that reports optimality after a time limit, infeasible status, or missing incumbent.

# Advanced MIP Patterns

## Use when

- The formulation needs logical implications, fixed charges, activation decisions, ordering, no-overlap, disjunctions, min/max, absolute values, tiers, route state, or resource calendars.
- A simple LP/MIP is producing unrealistic behavior because decisions are not linked tightly enough.
- The model is slow or infeasible and the likely cause is weak big-M logic, excessive pairwise variables, or missing state transitions.

## Do not use when

- A continuous LP is sufficient and adding binaries would not represent a real decision.
- The task is better handled by a specialized CP-SAT, constraint programming, nonlinear, or simulation method and the user did not require MIP.
- The user only needs a high-level explanation; do not introduce advanced constructs unless they clarify the actual model.

## General boundary rules

- Add binaries only for real yes/no choices, disjunctions, or nonconvex structure.
- Prefer the tightest formulation that matches the business rule; avoid decorative binaries.
- Use solver-native constructs when they improve clarity and are supported by the target environment.
- If a construct makes the model much slower, compare it with a simpler valid alternative before keeping it.

## Fixed charge and activation

- Use a binary `y` for whether an activity/resource is active.
- Link activity with a tight bound: `x <= U * y`.
- Add lower activation only when the business requires minimum use: `x >= L * y`.
- Charge fixed cost once through `fixed_cost * y`, not per unit.
- Do not force `y = 1` whenever `x = 0` unless the activity is mandatory.
- If there are startup/shutdown costs over time, separate active state from transition state.

## Logical rules

- Prefer solver-native indicator constraints for simple if-then rules when they keep the model clearer.
- If using big-M, derive M from data bounds and keep one M per rule family when possible.
- For either-or choices, use binaries whose sum is exactly one or at most one, matching the business rule.
- Distinguish implication (`A -> B`) from equivalence (`A <-> B`); the reverse direction often needs a separate constraint.
- For conditional capacity, link both activity and resource use to the condition.

## Min, max, absolute value

- For `z = max(a, b)`, add `z >= a`, `z >= b`, and objective pressure if minimizing z. Use binaries only when equality is needed without objective pressure.
- For absolute deviation `d = |x - target|`, add `d >= x - target` and `d >= target - x`.
- For service-level penalties, separate hard feasibility from soft penalty variables.
- For `min`, either transform with signs or add binaries when equality is not enforced by the objective.
- For ratio constraints, cross-multiply only when denominators are constant or safely bounded away from zero.

## Sequencing and no-overlap

- For jobs sharing a resource, use order binary `before[i,j]`.
- Typical pattern:
  - `start[j] >= end[i] - M * (1 - before[i,j])`
  - `start[i] >= end[j] - M * before[i,j]`
- Compute M from the scheduling horizon, not from an arbitrary large number.
- Include setup, cleanup, travel, rest, or turnover time in the correct side of the ordering constraint.
- Create pairwise order variables only for pairs that can share a resource or conflict.
- If time is discrete and the horizon is small, a time-indexed model may be cleaner than pairwise disjunctions.

## Piecewise and tiers

- Use solver-native piecewise-linear features for convex piecewise costs when suitable.
- For nonconvex tiers, use explicit segment variables and segment activation binaries.
- Ensure tier quantities sum to the original activity and each tier has its own upper bound.
- Distinguish incremental tiers from all-units tiers; their formulations and costs differ.
- Check whether discount tiers create nonconvex incentives that require binary selection.

## State and inventory logic

- State variables need initial conditions, transition equations, and terminal requirements if the business specifies them.
- Route or schedule state should be advanced in event order, not checked only row by row.
- Recovery, restock, cleaning, cooldown, maintenance, and rest rules often require cumulative state or last-event logic.
- If state can reset, model the reset trigger and its resource/time cost.

## Debugging advanced models

- First solve a relaxed or smaller instance and inspect whether the intended binaries activate.
- Add named constraints so IIS output points to business rules.
- If the reference solution is infeasible, replay the relevant state transitions manually before changing the evaluator.
- If the LP relaxation is very weak, inspect big-M values, missing lower/upper links, and symmetry.
- If the model is unexpectedly infeasible, test each advanced rule family on a tiny instance with a known feasible solution.

# Climate Refuge Reserve Selection

A coastal conservation agency is planning a portfolio of wetland parcels for climate adaptation. Each selected parcel receives one action: protection, restoration, or migration easement. The portfolio must be chosen before the agency knows how severe sea-level stress, salinity shift, restoration success, and development pressure will be in the planning year.

The goal is to maximize expected conservation value plus a reward for a strong worst-scenario conservation floor, net of capital and maintenance burden.

You are helping a coastal conservation agency choose a one-year portfolio of wetland and upland-migration parcels. The agency can protect, restore, or secure migration easements on selected parcels before knowing which sea-level, salinity, restoration-success, and development-pressure scenario will occur.

## Data

- `parcels.csv`: candidate parcels with region, owner type, area, marsh and upland-migration indices, acquisition cost, maintenance cost, staff effort, salinity resilience, corridor flag, public-access flag, and action eligibility.
- `actions.csv`: action-specific cost, maintenance, staff, habitat, upland, and nursery multipliers.
- `features.csv`: conservation features such as taxa or ecosystem-service targets, their home region, value, vulnerability weight, base target, and priority minimum-rate requirement.
- `scenarios.csv`: climate and land-use scenarios with probabilities and stress multipliers.
- `parcel_feature_habitat.csv`: sparse parcel-to-feature habitat contribution links with base habitat units and connectivity factors.
- `parcel_conflicts.csv`: parcel pairs that cannot both be selected because of negotiation-window or access-road conflicts.
- `region_requirements.csv`: minimum and maximum selected parcels, public-access requirements, and staff-day caps by implementation region.
- `config.json`: global budgets, corridor requirement, selected-parcel bounds, robust-floor weight, and scoring settings.

## Operating Rules

Each selected parcel must use exactly one eligible action. Unselected parcels should not appear in the final plan.

The portfolio must respect the capital budget, annual maintenance budget, total staff-day budget, restoration nursery-unit budget, minimum and maximum selected-parcel counts, and minimum number of selected migration-corridor parcels.

The agency also has implementation-balance rules: the portfolio must include at least the configured number of permanent protection actions and migration-easement actions, while restoration actions cannot exceed the configured maximum. These rules reflect legal permanence, future marsh migration needs, and limited contractor capacity for restoration work.

Each region has a minimum and maximum number of selected parcels, a minimum number of selected public-access parcels, and a staff-day cap. Parcel pairs listed in `parcel_conflicts.csv` cannot both be selected.

For each scenario, selected parcel-action pairs contribute habitat units to conservation features using the parcel-feature link, the action multipliers, parcel salinity resilience, and scenario stress multipliers. A feature is counted as covered in a scenario only when its accumulated habitat units reach the scenario-adjusted target.

Features carrying a priority coverage obligation are evaluated together in every scenario: the conservation value of the covered priority features must reach the required share of total priority-feature value. The plan also receives a robust-floor reward based on the weakest scenario's covered conservation value.

## Submission Schema

Write `submissions/solution.csv` with exactly these columns:

```csv
parcel_id,action
```

Include one row for each selected parcel. `action` must be one of `protect`, `restore`, or `migration_easement`, and it must be allowed for that parcel. Do not include duplicate parcel rows.

# Climate Refuge Reserve Selection Model

This instance is best represented as a binary first-stage assignment and scenario-covering MIP, not as a network flow, routing, or time-indexed model. The only here-and-now decisions are which parcels to select and which single action to assign to each selected parcel. Scenario dependence appears only in feature coverage and the worst-scenario floor, so a deterministic equivalent with scenario-specific coverage binaries is the strongest direct formulation for this scale.

## Sets and indices

- \(P\): parcels.
- \(A=\{\text{protect}, \text{restore}, \text{migration\_easement}\}\): actions.
- \(S\): scenarios.
- \(F\): conservation features.
- \(R\): implementation regions.
- \(E \subseteq P \times A\): eligible parcel-action pairs.
- \(L \subseteq P \times F\): parcel-feature habitat links.
- \(C \subseteq P \times P\): unordered parcel-conflict pairs.
- \(F^{pri}=\{f \in F : \rho_f > 0\}\): priority features.

## Parameters

From `parcels.csv`:

- \(r_p \in R\): region of parcel \(p\).
- \(u_p\): acquisition cost.
- \(m_p\): annual maintenance cost.
- \(d_p\): staff days.
- \(\mu_p\): current marsh index.
- \(\nu_p\): upland migration index.
- \(\sigma_p\): salinity resilience.
- \(\kappa_p \in \{0,1\}\): migration corridor indicator.
- \(q_p \in \{0,1\}\): public-access indicator.

From `actions.csv`:

- \(\alpha_a\): fixed-cost multiplier.
- \(\beta_a\): maintenance multiplier.
- \(\gamma_a\): staff multiplier.
- \(\eta_a\): habitat multiplier.
- \(\upsilon_a\): upland multiplier.
- \(\nabla_a\): nursery units.

From `features.csv`:

# Climate Refuge Reserve Selection Model

This instance is best represented as a binary first-stage assignment and scenario-covering MIP, not as a network flow, routing, or time-indexed model. The only here-and-now decisions are which parcels to select and which single action to assign to each selected parcel. Scenario dependence appears only in feature coverage and the worst-scenario floor, so a deterministic equivalent with scenario-specific coverage binaries is the strongest direct formulation for this scale.

## Sets and indices

- \(P\): parcels.
- \(A=\{\text{protect}, \text{restore}, \text{migration\_easement}\}\): actions.
- \(S\): scenarios.
- \(F\): conservation features.
- \(R\): implementation regions.
- \(E \subseteq P \times A\): eligible parcel-action pairs.
- \(L \subseteq P \times F\): parcel-feature habitat links.
- \(C \subseteq P \times P\): unordered parcel-conflict pairs.
- \(F^{pri}=\{f \in F : \rho_f > 0\}\): priority features.

## Parameters

From `parcels.csv`:

- \(r_p \in R\): region of parcel \(p\).
- \(u_p\): acquisition cost.
- \(m_p\): annual maintenance cost.
- \(d_p\): staff days.
- \(\mu_p\): current marsh index.
- \(\nu_p\): upland migration index.
- \(\sigma_p\): salinity resilience.
- \(\kappa_p \in \{0,1\}\): migration corridor indicator.
- \(q_p \in \{0,1\}\): public-access indicator.

From `actions.csv`:

- \(\alpha_a\): fixed-cost multiplier.
- \(\beta_a\): maintenance multiplier.
- \(\gamma_a\): staff multiplier.
- \(\eta_a\): habitat multiplier.
- \(\upsilon_a\): upland multiplier.
- \(\nabla_a\): nursery units.

From `features.csv`:

- \(v_f\): conservation value.
- \(\omega_f\): vulnerability weight.
- \(t_f\): base target units.
- \(\rho_f\): priority minimum-rate requirement.

From `scenarios.csv`:

- \(\pi_s\): scenario probability.
- \(sl_s\): sea-level stress multiplier.
- \(dp_s\): development-pressure multiplier.
- \(sa_s\): salinity-shift multiplier.
- \(rs_s\): restoration-success multiplier.

From `parcel_feature_habitat.csv`:

- \(h_{pf}\): base habitat units for \((p,f) \in L\).
- \(g_{pf}\): connectivity factor for \((p,f) \in L\).

From `region_requirements.csv`:

- \(L^R_r,U^R_r\): min/max selected parcels in region \(r\).
- \(Q_r\): min selected public-access parcels in region \(r\).
- \(D_r\): staff-day cap in region \(r\).

From `config.json`:

- \(B^{cap}\): capital budget.
- \(B^{mnt}\): annual maintenance budget.
- \(B^{staff}\): total staff-day budget.
- \(B^{nur}\): nursery-unit budget.
- \(L^P,U^P\): min/max selected parcels.
- \(L^{cor}\): minimum migration-corridor parcels.
- \(L^{prot}\): minimum protection actions.
- \(L^{ease}\): minimum migration-easement actions.
- \(U^{rest}\): maximum restoration actions.
- \(\lambda\): robust-floor weight.
- \(\phi\): benefit scale.
- \(\psi\): cost scale.

Derived action-adjusted resource coefficients for \((p,a)\in E\):

- Capital cost: \(K_{pa}=u_p \alpha_a\).
- Maintenance cost: \(M_{pa}=m_p \beta_a\).
- Staff use: \(D_{pa}=d_p \gamma_a\).
- Nursery use: \(N_{pa}=\nabla_a\).

### Scenario contribution and target assumptions

To keep the solver aligned with the public statement, the habitat contribution of a selected parcel-action pair is modeled as:

\[
c_{pfas}
=
h_{pf} g_{pf}
\left(
\frac{\mu_p \eta_a}{sl_s}
+
\frac{\nu_p \upsilon_a}{dp_s}
\right)
\cdot \frac{\sigma_p}{sa_s}
\cdot \chi_{a,s}
\]

where

\[
\chi_{a,s} =
\begin{cases}
rs_s & \text{if } a=\text{restore}\\
1 & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}
\]

and the intended multiplicative interpretation used in implementation is

\[
c_{pfas} =
h_{pf} g_{pf}
\left(
\frac{\mu_p \eta_a}{sl_s}
+
\frac{\nu_p \upsilon_a}{dp_s}
\right)
\cdot \frac{\sigma_p}{sa_s}
\cdot \chi_{a,s}.
\]

The scenario-adjusted target is

\[
T_{fs}
=
t_f
\left[
1 + (\omega_f-1)\left(\frac{sl_s+dp_s+sa_s}{3}-1\right)
\right].
\]

These assumptions use every public field that is operationally described in the statement. Descriptive metadata such as parcel coordinates, owner type, taxon group, and feature home region are not decision-driving in the public rules and are therefore not used directly in the MIP.

## Decision variables

- \(x_{pa} \in \{0,1\}\) for \((p,a)\in E\): 1 if parcel \(p\) is selected with action \(a\).
- \(z_{fs} \in \{0,1\}\) for \(f \in F, s \in S\): 1 if feature \(f\) is covered in scenario \(s\).
- \(w \ge 0\): worst-scenario covered conservation value floor.

## Objective

Let scenario score

\[
V_s = \sum_{f \in F} v_f z_{fs}.
\]

Maximize expected covered conservation value plus the robust floor reward, net of capital and maintenance burden:

\[
\max \quad
\phi \left(\sum_{s \in S} \pi_s V_s + \lambda w \right)
- \psi \sum_{(p,a)\in E} (K_{pa}+M_{pa}) x_{pa}.
\]

## Constraints

### 1. One action per selected parcel

\[
\sum_{a:(p,a)\in E} x_{pa} \le 1 \qquad \forall p \in P
\]

### 2. Global selected-parcel bounds

\[
L^P \le \sum_{(p,a)\in E} x_{pa} \le U^P
\]

### 3. Global budgets

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} K_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{cap}
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} M_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{mnt}
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} D_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{staff}
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} N_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{nur}
\]

### 4. Action-balance rules

\[
\sum_{p:(p,\text{protect})\in E} x_{p,\text{protect}} \ge L^{prot}
\]

\[
\sum_{p:(p,\text{migration\_easement})\in E} x_{p,\text{migration\_easement}} \ge L^{ease}
\]

\[
\sum_{p:(p,\text{restore})\in E} x_{p,\text{restore}} \le U^{rest}
\]

### 5. Corridor minimum

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} \kappa_p x_{pa} \ge L^{cor}
\]

### 6. Region selection and public-access rules

\[
L^R_r \le \sum_{(p,a)\in E: r_p=r} x_{pa} \le U^R_r
\qquad \forall r \in R
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E: r_p=r} q_p x_{pa} \ge Q_r
\qquad \forall r \in R
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E: r_p=r} D_{pa} x_{pa} \le D_r
\qquad \forall r \in R
\]

### 7. Parcel conflicts

For every \((p,p') \in C\),

\[
\sum_{a:(p,a)\in E} x_{pa}
+\sum_{a:(p',a)\in E} x_{p'a}
\le 1.
\]

### 8. Scenario-feature coverage

For every \(f \in F, s \in S\),

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E:(p,f)\in L} c_{pfas} x_{pa} \ge T_{fs} z_{fs}.
\]

### 9. Priority-feature conservation obligation

For every \(s \in S\),

\[
\sum_{f \in F^{pri}} v_f z_{fs}
\ge
\sum_{f \in F^{pri}} \rho_f v_f.
\]

---
name: or-pyscipopt
description: Use when building, running, validating, or debugging Python optimization models that use PySCIPOpt and SCIP.
metadata:
  short-description: Build and run PySCIPOpt models
---

# OR PySCIPOpt

## Use when

- The task asks for a Python optimization model using SCIP or `pyscipopt`.
- Existing solver code imports `pyscipopt`, sets SCIP parameters, writes `.lp`/`.mps`, or calls a SCIP-based checker.
- The user asks to run, debug, speed up, validate, or explain a SCIP-based solve.

## Do not use when

- The task only needs a mathematical formulation and no solver-specific code.
- The project clearly uses a different solver stack, such as Pyomo with CBC/HiGHS, OR-Tools CP-SAT, PuLP, CPLEX, or cvxpy, unless the user asks to port it to PySCIPOpt.
- The model is nonlinear or stochastic in a way that SCIP cannot handle directly; first decide whether it can be linearized or approximated.

## Environment rule

Use the Python environment specified by the user or the project. In this ORClaw workspace, the default solver environment is the `or` conda environment with PySCIPOpt available:

```bash
conda run -n or python <script.py>
```

If `pyscipopt` is missing, report the missing dependency, identify the command that failed, and try the project-specified environment before changing solver stacks.

## Workflow

1. Read the task brief and input files before coding.
2. Write the full mathematical model first: sets, parameters, decision variables, objective, and constraints.
3. Implement the model with explicit tuple keys and named constraints.
4. Solve with the selected Python command.
5. Export the solution using the exact schema required by the task.
6. Validate with the task checker or an independent recomputation of feasibility and objective.
7. If the solution is not accepted, diagnose whether the issue is formulation, data parsing, export formatting, tolerance, or evaluator mismatch before changing the objective or constraints.

## PySCIPOpt patterns

- Use `from pyscipopt import Model, quicksum`.
- Create sparse variable dictionaries explicitly, for example:

```python
x = {(i, j, t): model.addVar(vtype="B", name=f"x[{i},{j},{t}]") for i, j, t in keys}
```

- Use `quicksum(...)` for large linear expressions.
- Use `model.addCons(expr <= rhs, name="capacity[k,t]")` for constraints.
- Use `model.setObjective(expr, "minimize")` or `model.setObjective(expr, "maximize")`.
- Set reproducibility and limits with SCIP parameters:

```python
model.setParam("limits/gap", 0.0005)
model.setParam("limits/time", time_limit)
model.setParam("display/verblevel", 0)
```

- After `model.optimize()`, inspect `model.getStatus()` and only read values if a solution exists.
- Read variable values with `model.getVal(x[key])`, not `x[key].X`.
- Read objective and bound with `model.getObjVal()` and `model.getDualbound()`.
- Read relative gap with `model.getGap()` when available.

## Modeling hygiene

- Calibrate every big-M from data. If a tight M is not obvious, derive a bound and document it in a short comment.
- Keep feasibility checks, objective recomputation, and reference-optimality comparison separate.
- Do not round exported decision values unless the schema explicitly requires it.
- For benchmark work, treat the evaluator or checker as the scoring authority and the reference solver as a validation baseline.
- Keep solver logs or a concise solve summary when the user needs auditability.
- Avoid changing data to make a model feasible unless the task is explicitly to design or repair an instance.

## Debugging boundaries

- If a model is infeasible, inspect hard lower bounds, conservation equations, and mutually exclusive binary logic before relaxing constraints.
- If objective values differ, recompute each objective component from exported decisions.
- If runtime is high, first remove dominated variables, tighten big-M values, add eligibility filters, and check whether binaries are really necessary.
- If SCIP returns an incumbent with a gap, say so clearly; do not call it exact optimality unless the status and gap support that claim.
- If an evaluator rejects a solution that the model considers feasible, replay the rejected rule directly from the exported file before changing solver code.

## Useful commands

```bash
python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
conda run -n or python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
<python-command> <solver-script.py>
<python-command> <checker-script.py> --solution <solution-file> --env-dir <task-dir>
```

    s = float(pr['staff_days']) * float(ar['staff_multiplier'])

    reg=pr['region']; reg_count[reg]+=1; reg_staff[reg]+=s
    if pr['public_access']=='1': reg_public[reg]+=1
    if pr['migration_corridor']=='1': corridor += 1
    nur += float(ar['nursery_units'])

        active_conflicts.append((c['parcel_id_1'], c['parcel_id_2'], c['reason']))
print('active_conflicts', active_conflicts[:20], 'count', len(active_conflicts))

        fid=f['feature_id']
        contrib=0.0
        for l in sel_links.get(fid, []):
            pid=l['parcel_id']
            act = next(r['action'] for r in sol if r['parcel_id']==pid)
            pr=P[pid]; ar=A[act]
            chi = rs if act=='restore' else 1.0
            contrib += float(l['base_habitat_units']) * float(l['connectivity_factor']) * ((float(pr['current_marsh_index'])*float(ar['habitat_multiplier'])/sl) + (float(pr['upland_migration_index'])*float(ar['upland_multiplier'])/dp)) * (float(pr['salinity_resilience'])/sa) * chi
        omega=float(f['vulnerability_weight']); base=float(f['base_target_units'])
        target = base * (1.0 + (omega - 1.0) * (((sl+dp+sa)/3.0) - 1.0))
        if contrib + 1e-9 >= target:
            val=float(f['conservation_value'])
            total += val
            covered.append(fid)
            if float(f['priority_min_rate'])>0:
                prio += val
    vals.append((s['scenario_id'], total, prio, covered))
    if worst is None or total < worst[1]: worst=(s['scenario_id'], total, prio)
    if prio + 1e-9 < priority_req:
        bad_scen.append((s['scenario_id'], prio))
exp=sum(float(s['probability'])*v[1] for s,v in zip(scenarios, vals))
obj=config['benefit_scale']*(exp + config['robust_floor_weight']*worst[1]) - config['cost_scale']*(cap+mnt)
print('expected', exp)
print('worst', worst)
print('objective', obj)
print('bad_scenarios', bad_scen[:10], 'count', len(bad_scen))
print('scenario_values_sample', vals[:5])

/bin/bash -c "conda run -n or python - <<'PY'
import csv, json, math, time
from collections import defaultdict
from pyscipopt import Model, quicksum
base='/app'

def read_csv(path):
    with open(path, newline='') as f:
        return list(csv.DictReader(f))
with open(base+'/data/config.json') as f: config=json.load(f)
parcels=read_csv(base+'/data/parcels.csv')
actions=read_csv(base+'/data/actions.csv')
features=read_csv(base+'/data/features.csv')
scenarios=read_csv(base+'/data/scenarios.csv')
links=read_csv(base+'/data/parcel_feature_habitat.csv')
conflicts=read_csv(base+'/data/parcel_conflicts.csv')
regions=read_csv(base+'/data/region_requirements.csv')
sol=read_csv(base+'/submissions/solution.csv')
P={r['parcel_id']:r for r in parcels}
A={r['action']:r for r in actions}
F={r['feature_id']:r for r in features}
R={r['region']:r for r in regions}
selected={r['parcel_id']:r['action'] for r in sol}
regions_list=[r['region'] for r in regions]
priority=[f['feature_id'] for f in features if float(f['priority_min_rate'])>0]
priority_req=sum(float(F[f]['priority_min_rate'])*float(F[f]['conservation_value']) for f in priority)

eligible=[]
parcel_to_x=defaultdict(list)
region_pairs=defaultdict(list)
public_region_pairs=defaultdict(list)
act_pairs=defaultdict(list)
corridor_pairs=[]
cap_coef={}; mnt_coef={}; staff_coef={}; nur_coef={}
for p,pr in P.items():
    for a,flag in [('protect','protect_allowed'),('restore','restore_allowed'),('migration_easement','easement_allowed')]:
        if pr[flag]=='1':
            key=(p,a); eligible.append(key); parcel_to_x[p].append(key); region_pairs[pr['region']].append(key); act_pairs[a].append(key)
            if pr['public_access']=='1': public_region_pairs[pr['region']].append(key)
            if pr['migration_corridor']=='1': corridor_pairs.append(key)
            ar=A[a]
            cap_coef[key]=float(pr['acquisition_cost'])*float(ar['fixed_cost_multiplier'])
            mnt_coef[key]=float(pr['annual_maintenance_cost'])*float(ar['maintenance_multiplier'])
            staff_coef[key]=float(pr['staff_days'])*float(ar['staff_multiplier'])
            nur_coef[key]=float(ar['nursery_units'])

# group links by feature and parcel
links_by_feature=defaultdict(list)
for l in links:
    links_by_feature[l['feature_id']].append(l)

# precompute coverage coefficients only where link exists
cov = {}
for s in scenarios:
    sid=s['scenario_id']; sl=float(s['sea_level_stress']); dp=float(s['development_pressure']); sa=float(s['salinity_shift']); rs=float(s['restoration_success'])
    for f in features:
        fid=f['feature_id']
        for l in links_by_feature.get(fid, []):
            p=l['parcel_id']
            pr=P[p]
            base_h=float(l['base_habitat_units'])
            conn=float(l['connectivity_factor'])
            for a in ['protect','restore','migration_easement']:
                key=(p,a)
                if key not in cap_coef:
                    continue
                ar=A[a]
                chi = rs if a=='restore' else 1.0
                coeff = base_h*conn*((float(pr['current_marsh_index'])*float(ar['habitat_multiplier'])/sl) + (float(pr['upland_migration_index'])*float(ar['upland_multiplier'])/dp))*(float(pr['salinity_resilience'])/sa)*chi
                if coeff:
                    cov[(fid,sid,key)] = coeff

# targets and scenario values
T={}
val={f['feature_id']:float(f['conservation_value']) for f in features}
for s in scenarios:
    sid=s['scenario_id']; sl=float(s['sea_level_stress']); dp=float(s['development_pressure']); sa=float(s['salinity_shift'])
    stress_avg=(sl+dp+sa)/3.0
    for f in features:
        omega=float(f['vulnerability_weight']); base_t=float(f['base_target_units'])
        T[(f['feature_id'], sid)] = base_t*(1.0+(omega-1.0)*(stress_avg-1.0))

m=Model('verify')
m.setParam('display/verblevel', 0)
m.setParam('limits/time', 120)
m.setParam('parallel/maxnthreads', 1)
# modest gap target; we only need a good incumbent or proof
m.setParam('limits/gap', 1e-6)

x={k:m.addVar(vtype='B', name=f\"x[{k[0]},{k[1]}]\") for k in eligible}
z={(f['feature_id'],s['scenario_id']):m.addVar(vtype='B', name=f\"z[{f['feature_id']},{s['scenario_id']}]\") for f in features for s in scenarios}
w=m.addVar(lb=0.0, vtype='C', name='w')

for p,keys in parcel_to_x.items():
    m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in keys) <= 1, name=f'one[{p}]')

m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in eligible) >= config['minimum_selected_parcels'], name='min_sel')
m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in eligible) <= config['maximum_selected_parcels'], name='max_sel')
m.addCons(quicksum(cap_coef[k]*x[k] for k in eligible) <= config['capital_budget'], name='cap')
m.addCons(quicksum(mnt_coef[k]*x[k] for k in eligible) <= config['annual_maintenance_budget'], name='mnt')
m.addCons(quicksum(staff_coef[k]*x[k] for k in eligible) <= config['staff_day_budget'], name='staff')
m.addCons(quicksum(nur_coef[k]*x[k] for k in eligible) <= config['nursery_unit_budget'], name='nur')
m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in act_pairs['protect']) >= config['minimum_protect_actions'], name='min_protect')
m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in act_pairs['migration_easement']) >= config['minimum_migration_easement_actions'], name='min_ease')
m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in act_pairs['restore']) <= config['maximum_restore_actions'], name='max_restore')
m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in corridor_pairs) >= config['minimum_migration_corridors'], name='min_corridor')

for rr in regions:
    r=rr['region']
    m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in region_pairs[r]) >= int(rr['min_selected']), name=f'reg_min[{r}]')
    m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in region_pairs[r]) <= int(rr['max_selected']), name=f'reg_max[{r}]')
    m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in public_region_pairs[r]) >= int(rr['min_public_access']), name=f'pub[{r}]')
    m.addCons(quicksum(staff_coef[k]*x[k] for k in region_pairs[r]) <= float(rr['staff_day_cap']), name=f'reg_staff[{r}]')

for c in conflicts:
    p1,p2=c['parcel_id_1'],c['parcel_id_2']
    m.addCons(quicksum(x[k] for k in parcel_to_x[p1]) + quicksum(x[k] for k in parcel_to_x[p2]) <= 1, name=f'conf[{p1},{p2}]')

for s in scenarios:
    sid=s['scenario_id']
    scen_val=[]
    prio_terms=[]
    for f in features:
        fid=f['feature_id']
        expr = quicksum(cov[(fid,sid,k)]*x[k] for k in eligible if (fid,sid,k) in cov)
        m.addCons(expr >= T[(fid,sid)]*z[(fid,sid)], name=f'cover[{fid},{sid}]')
        scen_val.append(val[fid]*z[(fid,sid)])
        if fid in priority:
            prio_terms.append(val[fid]*z[(fid,sid)])
    Vs = quicksum(scen_val)
    m.addCons(quicksum(prio_terms) >= priority_req, name=f'priority[{sid}]')
    m.addCons(w <= Vs, name=f'worst[{sid}]')

obj = config['benefit_scale']*(quicksum(float(s['probability'])*quicksum(val[f['feature_id']]*z[(f['feature_id'], s['scenario_id'])] for f in features) for s in scenarios) + config['robust_floor_weight']*w) - config['cost_scale']*quicksum((cap_coef[k]+mnt_coef[k])*x[k] for k in eligible)
m.setObjective(obj, 'maximize')

# warm start from submitted solution
start = m.createSol()
for k,var in x.items():
    m.setSolVal(start, var, 1.0 if selected.get(k[0])==k[1] else 0.0)
# derive start z/w from submitted solution
# compute scenario coverage for submitted solution
for s in scenarios:
    sid=s['scenario_id']; sl=float(s['sea_level_stress']); dp=float(s['development_pressure']); sa=float(s['salinity_shift']); rs=float(s['restoration_success'])
    scen_total=0.0
    for f in features:
        fid=f['feature_id']
        expr=0.0
        for l in links_by_feature.get(fid, []):
            p=l['parcel_id']
            a=selected.get(p)
            if not a: continue
            expr += cov[(fid,sid,(p,a))]
        covered = 1.0 if expr + 1e-9 >= T[(fid,sid)] else 0.0
        m.setSolVal(start, z[(fid,sid)], covered)
        scen_total += val[fid]*covered
    # w will be set after loop
# compute worst
worst=min(sum(val[f['feature_id']]*(1.0 if sum(cov[(f['feature_id'],s['scenario_id'],(p,a))] for p,a in selected.items() if (f['feature_id'],s['scenario_id'],(p,a)) in cov) + 1e-9 >= T[(f['feature_id'],s['scenario_id'])] else 0.0) for f in features) for s in scenarios)
m.setSolVal(start, w, worst)
m.addSol(start, free=True)

start_time=time.time()
m.optimize()
runtime=time.time()-start_time
status=m.getStatus()
print('status', status)
print('runtime', runtime)
print('nsols', m.getNSols())
if m.getNSols() > 0:
    print('obj', m.getObjVal())
    try:
        print('dualbound', m.getDualbound())
        print('gap', m.getGap())
    except Exception as e:
        print('bound_error', e)
    chosen=[k for k in eligible if m.getVal(x[k]) > 0.5]
    print('chosen_count', len(chosen))
    print('chosen_sample', chosen[:20])
    selmap={p:a for p,a in chosen}
    # diff against submitted
    removed=sorted((p,a) for p,a in selected.items() if selmap.get(p)"'!=a)
    added=sorted((p,a) for p,a in selmap.items() if selected.get(p)!=a)
    print('"'removed_count', len(removed), 'added_count', len(added))
    print('removed_sample', removed[:20])
    print('added_sample', added[:20])

# OR PySCIPOpt

## Use when

- The task asks for a Python optimization model using SCIP or `pyscipopt`.
- Existing solver code imports `pyscipopt`, sets SCIP parameters, writes `.lp`/`.mps`, or calls a SCIP-based checker.
- The user asks to run, debug, speed up, validate, or explain a SCIP-based solve.

## Do not use when

- The task only needs a mathematical formulation and no solver-specific code.
- The project clearly uses a different solver stack, such as Pyomo with CBC/HiGHS, OR-Tools CP-SAT, PuLP, CPLEX, or cvxpy, unless the user asks to port it to PySCIPOpt.
- The model is nonlinear or stochastic in a way that SCIP cannot handle directly; first decide whether it can be linearized or approximated.

## Environment rule

Use the Python environment specified by the user or the project. In this ORClaw workspace, the default solver environment is the `or` conda environment with PySCIPOpt available:

```bash
conda run -n or python <script.py>
```

If `pyscipopt` is missing, report the missing dependency, identify the command that failed, and try the project-specified environment before changing solver stacks.

## Workflow

1. Read the task brief and input files before coding.
2. Write the full mathematical model first: sets, parameters, decision variables, objective, and constraints.
3. Implement the model with explicit tuple keys and named constraints.
4. Solve with the selected Python command.
5. Export the solution using the exact schema required by the task.
6. Validate with the task checker or an independent recomputation of feasibility and objective.
7. If the solution is not accepted, diagnose whether the issue is formulation, data parsing, export formatting, tolerance, or evaluator mismatch before changing the objective or constraints.

## PySCIPOpt patterns

- Use `from pyscipopt import Model, quicksum`.
- Create sparse variable dictionaries explicitly, for example:

```python
x = {(i, j, t): model.addVar(vtype="B", name=f"x[{i},{j},{t}]") for i, j, t in keys}
```

- Use `quicksum(...)` for large linear expressions.
- Use `model.addCons(expr <= rhs, name="capacity[k,t]")` for constraints.
- Use `model.setObjective(expr, "minimize")` or `model.setObjective(expr, "maximize")`.
- Set reproducibility and limits with SCIP parameters:

```python
model.setParam("limits/gap", 0.0005)
model.setParam("limits/time", time_limit)
model.setParam("display/verblevel", 0)
```

- After `model.optimize()`, inspect `model.getStatus()` and only read values if a solution exists.
- Read variable values with `model.getVal(x[key])`, not `x[key].X`.
- Read objective and bound with `model.getObjVal()` and `model.getDualbound()`.
- Read relative gap with `model.getGap()` when available.

## Modeling hygiene

- Calibrate every big-M from data. If a tight M is not obvious, derive a bound and document it in a short comment.
- Keep feasibility checks, objective recomputation, and reference-optimality comparison separate.
- Do not round exported decision values unless the schema explicitly requires it.
- For benchmark work, treat the evaluator or checker as the scoring authority and the reference solver as a validation baseline.
- Keep solver logs or a concise solve summary when the user needs auditability.
- Avoid changing data to make a model feasible unless the task is explicitly to design or repair an instance.

## Debugging boundaries

- If a model is infeasible, inspect hard lower bounds, conservation equations, and mutually exclusive binary logic before relaxing constraints.
- If objective values differ, recompute each objective component from exported decisions.
- If runtime is high, first remove dominated variables, tighten big-M values, add eligibility filters, and check whether binaries are really necessary.
- If SCIP returns an incumbent with a gap, say so clearly; do not call it exact optimality unless the status and gap support that claim.
- If an evaluator rejects a solution that the model considers feasible, replay the rejected rule directly from the exported file before changing solver code.

## Useful commands

```bash
python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
conda run -n or python -c "import pyscipopt; print(pyscipopt.__version__)"
<python-command> <solver-script.py>
<python-command> <checker-script.py> --solution <solution-file> --env-dir <task-dir>
```

# Optimization Modeling Core

## Use when

- The user gives a word problem, business process, paper scenario, or benchmark prompt and expects a formal optimization model.
- Solver code exists but the issue appears to be a missing or incorrect formulation.
- The task asks for a complete model, not only a heuristic, simulation, or data summary.

## Do not use when

- The task is purely about software plumbing, visualization, data cleaning, or report writing.
- The problem is already fully formulated and the user only asks for code translation into a specific solver; use the solver-specific skill as the primary guide.
- The request is exploratory and the user explicitly wants brainstorming without implementation.

## Model-first workflow

1. Restate the business decision in plain language.
2. Identify sets, indices, units, and time granularity.
3. Extract parameters from data files and confirm units.
4. Define decision variables with domains and business meaning.
5. Write the objective with every cost, reward, penalty, and service term.
6. Write constraints grouped by operational rule.
7. Check edge cases: missing eligibility, impossible demand, fixed commitments, and terminal-state requirements.
8. Only then implement code.

## Ambiguity handling

- If the brief is ambiguous but data and examples imply a convention, state the assumption and continue.
- If ambiguity changes feasibility, objective direction, or deliverable schema, ask before coding or implement the least risky interpretation with a clear note.
- If units conflict across files, normalize units explicitly before modeling.
- If a hard requirement conflicts with data, diagnose the conflict before adding slack variables.

## Formulation checklist

- Every row in an input table should either become a parameter, an eligibility rule, or be explicitly irrelevant.
- Every decision variable should appear in at least one binding constraint and usually in the objective.
- Demand balance should use the correct equality or inequality direction; do not weaken equality to `>=` unless surplus is genuinely allowed.
- Capacity constraints should include activation/open/setup binaries when the business says inactive resources cannot produce, ship, or serve.
- Shortage, backlog, overtime, outsourcing, and postponement variables need explicit cost and bounds.
- Multi-period models need state transition equations and initial/terminal states.
- If the user asks for explanation, describe the modeling logic in business terms after solving.

## Constraint boundary rules

- Hard constraints represent physical impossibility, policy mandates, legal requirements, or explicit benchmark feasibility rules.
- Soft constraints need penalty variables or objective terms; do not silently turn hard constraints into penalties.
- Service levels should specify numerator, denominator, eligible population, and time window.
- Fairness constraints should define the comparison group and whether unavailable resources count.
- Logical implications need both directions only when the business rule says equivalence, not merely implication.

## Objective boundary rules

- Confirm whether the problem minimizes cost, maximizes profit, maximizes service, or uses lexicographic priorities.
- Do not mix penalties with primary economics unless their scale is intentional.
- Include fixed costs only once per activated entity.
- Include revenue only for fulfilled demand or accepted jobs, not for unmet or canceled work.
- Keep reporting metrics separate from optimized objective terms unless the prompt explicitly optimizes them.

## Implementation expectations

- Use structured parsing for CSV/JSON instead of manual string manipulation.
- Keep solver code, solution export, and validation code clearly separated.
- For benchmark or task environments, read the provided brief before coding and follow the expected output schema exactly.
- When comparing two answers, diagnose concrete formulation differences and objective consequences, not only feasibility.
- Write exported solutions at the precision expected by the checker; avoid unnecessary rounding.
- Preserve the input schema and output schema unless the user asks to redesign the benchmark.

## Verification boundary

- Verify feasibility independently from solver status when an evaluator or checker exists.
- Recompute objective from exported artifacts, not only from in-memory solver expressions.
- For benchmark tasks, create at least one negative check for each newly added rule when practical.
- For paper or real-world tasks, separate what is directly supported by the source from synthetic or assumed data.

## Red flags

- Objective terms that are counted twice or charged at the wrong marginal rate.
- Binary variables not linked tightly enough to continuous activity.
- Time periods shifted by one index.
- Average or fairness constraints that ignore the denominator or eligibility set.
- Public prompt files that reveal solver-ready variables or formulas when the benchmark is meant to test modeling translation.
- A feasible solution that exploits missing eligibility, free disposal, negative cycles, or unbounded production.
- A model that reports optimality after a time limit, infeasible status, or missing incumbent.

# Climate Refuge Reserve Selection

A coastal conservation agency is planning a portfolio of wetland parcels for climate adaptation. Each selected parcel receives one action: protection, restoration, or migration easement. The portfolio must be chosen before the agency knows how severe sea-level stress, salinity shift, restoration success, and development pressure will be in the planning year.

The goal is to maximize expected conservation value plus a reward for a strong worst-scenario conservation floor, net of capital and maintenance burden.

You are helping a coastal conservation agency choose a one-year portfolio of wetland and upland-migration parcels. The agency can protect, restore, or secure migration easements on selected parcels before knowing which sea-level, salinity, restoration-success, and development-pressure scenario will occur.

## Data

- `parcels.csv`: candidate parcels with region, owner type, area, marsh and upland-migration indices, acquisition cost, maintenance cost, staff effort, salinity resilience, corridor flag, public-access flag, and action eligibility.
- `actions.csv`: action-specific cost, maintenance, staff, habitat, upland, and nursery multipliers.
- `features.csv`: conservation features such as taxa or ecosystem-service targets, their home region, value, vulnerability weight, base target, and priority minimum-rate requirement.
- `scenarios.csv`: climate and land-use scenarios with probabilities and stress multipliers.
- `parcel_feature_habitat.csv`: sparse parcel-to-feature habitat contribution links with base habitat units and connectivity factors.
- `parcel_conflicts.csv`: parcel pairs that cannot both be selected because of negotiation-window or access-road conflicts.
- `region_requirements.csv`: minimum and maximum selected parcels, public-access requirements, and staff-day caps by implementation region.
- `config.json`: global budgets, corridor requirement, selected-parcel bounds, robust-floor weight, and scoring settings.

## Operating Rules

Each selected parcel must use exactly one eligible action. Unselected parcels should not appear in the final plan.

The portfolio must respect the capital budget, annual maintenance budget, total staff-day budget, restoration nursery-unit budget, minimum and maximum selected-parcel counts, and minimum number of selected migration-corridor parcels.

The agency also has implementation-balance rules: the portfolio must include at least the configured number of permanent protection actions and migration-easement actions, while restoration actions cannot exceed the configured maximum. These rules reflect legal permanence, future marsh migration needs, and limited contractor capacity for restoration work.

Each region has a minimum and maximum number of selected parcels, a minimum number of selected public-access parcels, and a staff-day cap. Parcel pairs listed in `parcel_conflicts.csv` cannot both be selected.

For each scenario, selected parcel-action pairs contribute habitat units to conservation features using the parcel-feature link, the action multipliers, parcel salinity resilience, and scenario stress multipliers. A feature is counted as covered in a scenario only when its accumulated habitat units reach the scenario-adjusted target.

Features carrying a priority coverage obligation are evaluated together in every scenario: the conservation value of the covered priority features must reach the required share of total priority-feature value. The plan also receives a robust-floor reward based on the weakest scenario's covered conservation value.

## Submission Schema

Write `submissions/solution.csv` with exactly these columns:

```csv
parcel_id,action
```

Include one row for each selected parcel. `action` must be one of `protect`, `restore`, or `migration_easement`, and it must be allowed for that parcel. Do not include duplicate parcel rows.

# Advanced MIP Patterns

## Use when

- The formulation needs logical implications, fixed charges, activation decisions, ordering, no-overlap, disjunctions, min/max, absolute values, tiers, route state, or resource calendars.
- A simple LP/MIP is producing unrealistic behavior because decisions are not linked tightly enough.
- The model is slow or infeasible and the likely cause is weak big-M logic, excessive pairwise variables, or missing state transitions.

## Do not use when

- A continuous LP is sufficient and adding binaries would not represent a real decision.
- The task is better handled by a specialized CP-SAT, constraint programming, nonlinear, or simulation method and the user did not require MIP.
- The user only needs a high-level explanation; do not introduce advanced constructs unless they clarify the actual model.

## General boundary rules

- Add binaries only for real yes/no choices, disjunctions, or nonconvex structure.
- Prefer the tightest formulation that matches the business rule; avoid decorative binaries.
- Use solver-native constructs when they improve clarity and are supported by the target environment.
- If a construct makes the model much slower, compare it with a simpler valid alternative before keeping it.

## Fixed charge and activation

- Use a binary `y` for whether an activity/resource is active.
- Link activity with a tight bound: `x <= U * y`.
- Add lower activation only when the business requires minimum use: `x >= L * y`.
- Charge fixed cost once through `fixed_cost * y`, not per unit.
- Do not force `y = 1` whenever `x = 0` unless the activity is mandatory.
- If there are startup/shutdown costs over time, separate active state from transition state.

## Logical rules

- Prefer solver-native indicator constraints for simple if-then rules when they keep the model clearer.
- If using big-M, derive M from data bounds and keep one M per rule family when possible.
- For either-or choices, use binaries whose sum is exactly one or at most one, matching the business rule.
- Distinguish implication (`A -> B`) from equivalence (`A <-> B`); the reverse direction often needs a separate constraint.
- For conditional capacity, link both activity and resource use to the condition.

## Min, max, absolute value

- For `z = max(a, b)`, add `z >= a`, `z >= b`, and objective pressure if minimizing z. Use binaries only when equality is needed without objective pressure.
- For absolute deviation `d = |x - target|`, add `d >= x - target` and `d >= target - x`.
- For service-level penalties, separate hard feasibility from soft penalty variables.
- For `min`, either transform with signs or add binaries when equality is not enforced by the objective.
- For ratio constraints, cross-multiply only when denominators are constant or safely bounded away from zero.

## Sequencing and no-overlap

- For jobs sharing a resource, use order binary `before[i,j]`.
- Typical pattern:
  - `start[j] >= end[i] - M * (1 - before[i,j])`
  - `start[i] >= end[j] - M * before[i,j]`
- Compute M from the scheduling horizon, not from an arbitrary large number.
- Include setup, cleanup, travel, rest, or turnover time in the correct side of the ordering constraint.
- Create pairwise order variables only for pairs that can share a resource or conflict.
- If time is discrete and the horizon is small, a time-indexed model may be cleaner than pairwise disjunctions.

## Piecewise and tiers

- Use solver-native piecewise-linear features for convex piecewise costs when suitable.
- For nonconvex tiers, use explicit segment variables and segment activation binaries.
- Ensure tier quantities sum to the original activity and each tier has its own upper bound.
- Distinguish incremental tiers from all-units tiers; their formulations and costs differ.
- Check whether discount tiers create nonconvex incentives that require binary selection.

## State and inventory logic

- State variables need initial conditions, transition equations, and terminal requirements if the business specifies them.
- Route or schedule state should be advanced in event order, not checked only row by row.
- Recovery, restock, cleaning, cooldown, maintenance, and rest rules often require cumulative state or last-event logic.
- If state can reset, model the reset trigger and its resource/time cost.

## Debugging advanced models

- First solve a relaxed or smaller instance and inspect whether the intended binaries activate.
- Add named constraints so IIS output points to business rules.
- If the reference solution is infeasible, replay the relevant state transitions manually before changing the evaluator.
- If the LP relaxation is very weak, inspect big-M values, missing lower/upper links, and symmetry.
- If the model is unexpectedly infeasible, test each advanced rule family on a tiny instance with a known feasible solution.

    links=list(csv.DictReader(f))

# feature link counts and totals

    p=parcels[r['parcel_id']]
    base_units=float(r['base_habitat_units'])*float(r['connectivity_factor'])
    cnt[r['feature_id']]+=1
    tot[r['feature_id']]+=base_units
print('link count range',min(cnt.values()),max(cnt.values()))
# sample target ratios ignoring parcel/action fields
ratios=[tot[f]/features[f]['base_target_units'] for f in features]
print('total base contribution / target range',min(ratios),max(ratios),'avg',sum(ratios)/len(ratios))
# action-weighted quality candidates
for formula in ['marsh_only','upland_only','marsh+upland','weighted_avg']:
    vals=[]
    for act,adata in actions.items():
        total=defaultdict(float)
        for r in links:
            pid=r['parcel_id']; p=parcels[pid]
            base_units=float(r['base_habitat_units'])*float(r['connectivity_factor'])
            if formula=='marsh_only':
                qual=p['current_marsh_index']*adata['habitat_multiplier']
            elif formula=='upland_only':
                qual=p['upland_migration_index']*adata['upland_multiplier']
            elif formula=='marsh+upland':
                qual=p['current_marsh_index']*adata['habitat_multiplier']+p['upland_migration_index']*adata['upland_multiplier']
            else:
                qual=(p['current_marsh_index']*adata['habitat_multiplier']+p['upland_migration_index']*adata['upland_multiplier'])/2
            total[r['feature_id']]+=base_units*qual
        ratios=[total[f]/features[f]['base_target_units'] for f in features]
        vals.append((act,min(ratios),sum(ratios)/len(ratios),max(ratios)))
    print(formula, vals)
# priority stats
priority=[f for f,d in features.items() if d['priority_min_rate']>0]
print('priority count',len(priority),'total priority value',sum(features[f]['conservation_value'] for f in priority))
print('min/max priority rate',min(features[f]['priority_min_rate'] for f in priority),max(features[f]['priority_min_rate'] for f in priority))

/bin/bash -lc "PYTHONPATH=/opt/or-deps python - <<'PY'
import csv, json
from collections import defaultdict
base='/app/data'
with open(f'{base}/actions.csv') as f:
    actions={r['action']:{k:float(v) for k,v in r.items() if k"'!='"'action'} for r in csv.DictReader(f)}
with open(f'{base}/parcels.csv') as f:
    parcels={r['parcel_id']:{k:(float(v) if k not in ('region','owner_type') else v) for k,v in r.items() if k"'!='"'parcel_id'} for r in csv.DictReader(f)}
with open(f'{base}/features.csv') as f:
    features={r['feature_id']:{k:(float(v) if k not in ('taxon_group','home_region') else v) for k,v in r.items() if k"'!='"'feature_id'} for r in csv.DictReader(f)}
with open(f'{base}/scenarios.csv') as f:
    scenarios=[{k:(float(v) if k"'!='"'scenario_id' else v) for k,v in r.items()} for r in csv.DictReader(f)]
with open(f'{base}/parcel_feature_habitat.csv') as f:
    links=list(csv.DictReader(f))

def target(fs, s, mode):
    f=features[fs]
    if mode=='base':
        return f['base_target_units']
    if mode=='vuln':
        return f['base_target_units']*f['vulnerability_weight']
    sev=(s['sea_level_stress']+s['development_pressure']+s['salinity_shift'])/3.0
    if mode=='base_sev':
        return f['base_target_units']*sev
    if mode=='vuln_delta':
        return f['base_target_units']*(1.0+(f['vulnerability_weight']-1.0)*(sev-1.0))
    if mode=='vuln_sev':
        return f['base_target_units']*f['vulnerability_weight']*sev

def contrib(pid, act, s, mode):
    p=parcels[pid]; a=actions[act]
    marsh=p['current_marsh_index']*a['habitat_multiplier']
    upland=p['upland_migration_index']*a['upland_multiplier']
    sal=max(0.0, p['salinity_resilience']/s['salinity_shift'])
    sea=1.0/s['sea_level_stress']
    dev=1.0/s['development_pressure']
    rest=s['restoration_success'] if act=='restore' else 1.0
    if mode=='sum_div':
        return marsh*sea*sal*rest + upland*dev
    if mode=='avg_div':
        return 0.5*(marsh*sea*sal*rest + upland*dev)
    if mode=='sum_nodiv':
        return (marsh+upland)*sal*rest
    if mode=='avg_nodiv':
        return 0.5*(marsh+upland)*sal*rest
    raise

for cmode in ['sum_div','avg_div','sum_nodiv','avg_nodiv']:
    for tmode in ['base','vuln','base_sev','vuln_delta','vuln_sev']:
        min_ratio=1e9; max_ratio=-1; avg=0; n=0
        for s in scenarios:
            for act in actions:
                totals=defaultdict(float)
                for r in links:
                    totals[r['feature_id']]+=float(r['base_habitat_units'])*float(r['connectivity_factor'])*contrib(r['parcel_id'],act,s,cmode)
                ratios=[totals[f]/target(f,s,tmode) for f in features]
                min_ratio=min(min_ratio,min(ratios)); max_ratio=max(max_ratio,max(ratios)); avg+=sum(ratios)/len(ratios); n+=1
        print(cmode,tmode,'min',round(min_ratio,3),'avg',round(avg/n,3),'max',round(max_ratio,3))
PY"

result57. Command output
# Climate Refuge Reserve Selection Model

This instance is best represented as a binary first-stage assignment and scenario-covering MIP, not as a network flow, routing, or time-indexed model. The only here-and-now decisions are which parcels to select and which single action to assign to each selected parcel. Scenario dependence appears only in feature coverage and the worst-scenario floor, so a deterministic equivalent with scenario-specific coverage binaries is the strongest direct formulation for this scale.

## Sets and indices

- \(P\): parcels.
- \(A=\{\text{protect}, \text{restore}, \text{migration\_easement}\}\): actions.
- \(S\): scenarios.
- \(F\): conservation features.
- \(R\): implementation regions.
- \(E \subseteq P \times A\): eligible parcel-action pairs.
- \(L \subseteq P \times F\): parcel-feature habitat links.
- \(C \subseteq P \times P\): unordered parcel-conflict pairs.
- \(F^{pri}=\{f \in F : \rho_f > 0\}\): priority features.

## Parameters

From `parcels.csv`:

- \(r_p \in R\): region of parcel \(p\).
- \(u_p\): acquisition cost.
- \(m_p\): annual maintenance cost.
- \(d_p\): staff days.
- \(\mu_p\): current marsh index.
- \(\nu_p\): upland migration index.
- \(\sigma_p\): salinity resilience.
- \(\kappa_p \in \{0,1\}\): migration corridor indicator.
- \(q_p \in \{0,1\}\): public-access indicator.

From `actions.csv`:

- \(\alpha_a\): fixed-cost multiplier.
- \(\beta_a\): maintenance multiplier.
- \(\gamma_a\): staff multiplier.
- \(\eta_a\): habitat multiplier.
- \(\upsilon_a\): upland multiplier.
- \(\nabla_a\): nursery units.

From `features.csv`:

- \(v_f\): conservation value.
- \(\omega_f\): vulnerability weight.
- \(t_f\): base target units.
- \(\rho_f\): priority minimum-rate requirement.

From `scenarios.csv`:

- \(\pi_s\): scenario probability.
- \(sl_s\): sea-level stress multiplier.
- \(dp_s\): development-pressure multiplier.
- \(sa_s\): salinity-shift multiplier.
- \(rs_s\): restoration-success multiplier.

From `parcel_feature_habitat.csv`:

- \(h_{pf}\): base habitat units for \((p,f) \in L\).
- \(g_{pf}\): connectivity factor for \((p,f) \in L\).

From `region_requirements.csv`:

- \(L^R_r,U^R_r\): min/max selected parcels in region \(r\).
- \(Q_r\): min selected public-access parcels in region \(r\).
- \(D_r\): staff-day cap in region \(r\).

From `config.json`:

- \(B^{cap}\): capital budget.
- \(B^{mnt}\): annual maintenance budget.
- \(B^{staff}\): total staff-day budget.
- \(B^{nur}\): nursery-unit budget.
- \(L^P,U^P\): min/max selected parcels.
- \(L^{cor}\): minimum migration-corridor parcels.
- \(L^{prot}\): minimum protection actions.
- \(L^{ease}\): minimum migration-easement actions.
- \(U^{rest}\): maximum restoration actions.
- \(\lambda\): robust-floor weight.
- \(\phi\): benefit scale.
- \(\psi\): cost scale.

Derived action-adjusted resource coefficients for \((p,a)\in E\):

- Capital cost: \(K_{pa}=u_p \alpha_a\).
- Maintenance cost: \(M_{pa}=m_p \beta_a\).
- Staff use: \(D_{pa}=d_p \gamma_a\).
- Nursery use: \(N_{pa}=\nabla_a\).

### Scenario contribution and target assumptions

To keep the solver aligned with the public statement, the habitat contribution of a selected parcel-action pair is modeled as:

\[
c_{pfas} = h_{pf} g_{pf}
\left(
\frac{\mu_p \eta_a}{sl_s}
 \frac{\nu_p \upsilon_a}{dp_s}
\right)
\frac{\sigma_p}{sa_s}
\chi_{a,s}
\]

where

\[
\chi_{a,s} =
\begin{cases}
rs_s & \text{if } a=\text{restore}\\
1 & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}
\]

and the intended multiplicative interpretation used in implementation is

\[
c_{pfas} =
h_{pf} g_{pf}
\left(
\frac{\mu_p \eta_a}{sl_s}
 \frac{\nu_p \upsilon_a}{dp_s}
\right)
\frac{\sigma_p}{sa_s}
\chi_{a,s}.
\]

The scenario-adjusted target is

\[
T_{fs}
=
t_f
\left[
1 + (\omega_f-1)\left(\frac{sl_s+dp_s+sa_s}{3}-1\right)
\right].
\]

These assumptions use every public field that is operationally described in the statement. Descriptive metadata such as parcel coordinates, owner type, taxon group, and feature home region are not decision-driving in the public rules and are therefore not used directly in the MIP.

## Decision variables

- \(x_{pa} \in \{0,1\}\) for \((p,a)\in E\): 1 if parcel \(p\) is selected with action \(a\).
- \(z_{fs} \in \{0,1\}\) for \(f \in F, s \in S\): 1 if feature \(f\) is covered in scenario \(s\).
- \(w \ge 0\): worst-scenario covered conservation value floor.

## Objective

Let scenario score

\[
V_s = \sum_{f \in F} v_f z_{fs}.
\]

Maximize expected covered conservation value plus the robust floor reward, net of capital and maintenance burden:

\[
\max \quad
\phi \left(\sum_{s \in S} \pi_s V_s + \lambda w \right)
- \psi \sum_{(p,a)\in E} (K_{pa}+M_{pa}) x_{pa}.
\]

## Constraints

### 1. One action per selected parcel

\[
\sum_{a:(p,a)\in E} x_{pa} \le 1 \qquad \forall p \in P
\]

### 2. Global selected-parcel bounds

\[
L^P \le \sum_{(p,a)\in E} x_{pa} \le U^P
\]

### 3. Global budgets

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} K_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{cap}
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} M_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{mnt}
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} D_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{staff}
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} N_{pa} x_{pa} \le B^{nur}
\]

### 4. Action-balance rules

\[
\sum_{p:(p,\text{protect})\in E} x_{p,\text{protect}} \ge L^{prot}
\]

\[
\sum_{p:(p,\text{migration\_easement})\in E} x_{p,\text{migration\_easement}} \ge L^{ease}
\]

\[
\sum_{p:(p,\text{restore})\in E} x_{p,\text{restore}} \le U^{rest}
\]

### 5. Corridor minimum

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E} \kappa_p x_{pa} \ge L^{cor}
\]

### 6. Region selection and public-access rules

\[
L^R_r \le \sum_{(p,a)\in E: r_p=r} x_{pa} \le U^R_r
\qquad \forall r \in R
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E: r_p=r} q_p x_{pa} \ge Q_r
\qquad \forall r \in R
\]

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E: r_p=r} D_{pa} x_{pa} \le D_r
\qquad \forall r \in R
\]

### 7. Parcel conflicts

For every \((p,p') \in C\),

\[
\sum_{a:(p,a)\in E} x_{pa}
+\sum_{a:(p',a)\in E} x_{p'a}
\le 1.
\]

### 8. Scenario-feature coverage

For every \(f \in F, s \in S\),

\[
\sum_{(p,a)\in E:(p,f)\in L} c_{pfas} x_{pa} \ge T_{fs} z_{fs}.
\]

### 9. Priority-feature conservation obligation

For every \(s \in S\),

\[
\sum_{f \in F^{pri}} v_f z_{fs}
\ge
\sum_{f \in F^{pri}} \rho_f v_f.
\]

This aggregates the per-feature minimum-rate requirements into the required covered share of total priority-feature value in each scenario.

### 10. Worst-scenario conservation floor

import csv
import json
import math
import os
import sys
import time
from collections import defaultdict
from typing import Dict, List, Tuple

from pyscipopt import Model, quicksum


BASE_DIR = "/app"
DATA_DIR = os.path.join(BASE_DIR, "data")
SUBMISSIONS_DIR = os.path.join(BASE_DIR, "submissions")
SOLUTION_PATH = os.path.join(SUBMISSIONS_DIR, "solution.csv")
LOG_PATH = os.path.join(SUBMISSIONS_DIR, "solve_log.md")

REL_GAP = 0.0005
MAX_SINGLE_ATTEMPT_SECONDS = 300.0
FEAS_TOL = 1e-6


def _read_csv(path: str) -> List[Dict[str, str]]:
    with open(path, "r", newline="") as fh:
        return list(csv.DictReader(fh))


def _float_fmt(value: float) -> str:
    if value is None or (isinstance(value, float) and (math.isinf(value) or math.isnan(value))):
        return "n/a"
    return f"{value:.8f}"


def _norm_residual_lte(lhs: float, rhs: float) -> float:
    return max(0.0, lhs - rhs) / max(1.0, abs(rhs))


def _norm_residual_gte(lhs: float, rhs: float) -> float:
    return max(0.0, rhs - lhs) / max(1.0, abs(rhs))


def get_time_limit() -> float:
    env_value = os.environ.get("ORCLAW_SOLVE_TIME_LIMIT_SECONDS")
    if env_value is None:
        return MAX_SINGLE_ATTEMPT_SECONDS
    try:
        parsed = float(env_value)
    except ValueError:
        return MAX_SINGLE_ATTEMPT_SECONDS
    return max(1.0, min(MAX_SINGLE_ATTEMPT_SECONDS, parsed))


def load_instance() -> Dict:
    with open(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "config.json"), "r") as fh:
        config = json.load(fh)

    action_rows = _read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "actions.csv"))
    parcel_rows = _read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "parcels.csv"))
    feature_rows = _read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "features.csv"))
    scenario_rows = _read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "scenarios.csv"))
    conflict_rows = _read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "parcel_conflicts.csv"))
    region_rows = _read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "region_requirements.csv"))
    link_rows = _read_csv(os.path.join(DATA_DIR, "parcel_feature_habitat.csv"))

    actions = {}
    for row in action_rows:
        act = row["action"]
        actions[act] = {
            "fixed_cost_multiplier": float(row["fixed_cost_multiplier"]),
            "maintenance_multiplier": float(row["maintenance_multiplier"]),
            "staff_multiplier": float(row["staff_multiplier"]),
            "habitat_multiplier": float(row["habitat_multiplier"]),
            "upland_multiplier": float(row["upland_multiplier"]),
            "nursery_units": float(row["nursery_units"]),
            "public_access_required": int(row["public_access_required"]),
        }

    parcels = {}
    for row in parcel_rows:
        pid = row["parcel_id"]
        parcels[pid] = {
            "region": row["region"],
            "owner_type": row["owner_type"],
            "x": float(row["x"]),
            "y": float(row["y"]),
            "area_ha": float(row["area_ha"]),
            "current_marsh_index": float(row["current_marsh_index"]),
            "upland_migration_index": float(row["upland_migration_index"]),
            "acquisition_cost": float(row["acquisition_cost"]),
            "annual_maintenance_cost": float(row["annual_maintenance_cost"]),
            "staff_days": float(row["staff_days"]),
            "salinity_resilience": float(row["salinity_resilience"]),
            "migration_corridor": int(row["migration_corridor"]),
            "public_access": int(row["public_access"]),
            "protect_allowed": int(row["protect_allowed"]),
            "restore_allowed": int(row["restore_allowed"]),
            "easement_allowed": int(row["easement_allowed"]),
        }

    features = {}
    for row in feature_rows:
        fid = row["feature_id"]
        features[fid] = {
            "taxon_group": row["taxon_group"],
            "home_region": row["home_region"],
            "conservation_value": float(row["conservation_value"]),
            "vulnerability_weight": float(row["vulnerability_weight"]),
            "base_target_units": float(row["base_target_units"]),
            "priority_min_rate": float(row["priority_min_rate"]),
        }

    scenarios = []
    for row in scenario_rows:
        scenarios.append(
            {
                "scenario_id": row["scenario_id"],
                "probability": float(row["probability"]),
                "sea_level_stress": float(row["sea_level_stress"]),
                "development_pressure": float(row["development_pressure"]),
                "salinity_shift": float(row["salinity_shift"]),
                "restoration_success": float(row["restoration_success"]),
            }
        )

    regions = {}
    for row in region_rows:
        region = row["region"]
        regions[region] = {
            "min_selected": int(row["min_selected"]),
            "max_selected": int(row["max_selected"]),
            "min_public_access": int(row["min_public_access"]),
            "staff_day_cap": float(row["staff_day_cap"]),
        }

    conflicts = [(row["parcel_id_1"], row["parcel_id_2"], row["reason"]) for row in conflict_rows]

    links_by_feature = defaultdict(list)
    link_value = {}
    for row in link_rows:
        pid = row["parcel_id"]
        fid = row["feature_id"]
        base_link = float(row["base_habitat_units"]) * float(row["connectivity_factor"])
        links_by_feature[fid].append((pid, base_link))
        link_value[(pid, fid)] = base_link

    eligible = []
    eligible_by_parcel = defaultdict(list)
    for pid, pdata in parcels.items():
        if pdata["protect_allowed"]:
            eligible.append((pid, "protect"))
            eligible_by_parcel[pid].append("protect")
        if pdata["restore_allowed"]:
            eligible.append((pid, "restore"))
            eligible_by_parcel[pid].append("restore")
        if pdata["easement_allowed"]:
            eligible.append((pid, "migration_easement"))
            eligible_by_parcel[pid].append("migration_easement")

    priority_features = [fid for fid, fdata in features.items() if fdata["priority_min_rate"] > 0.0]

    return {
        "config": config,
        "actions": actions,
        "parcels": parcels,
        "features": features,
        "scenarios": scenarios,
        "regions": regions,
        "conflicts": conflicts,
        "links_by_feature": dict(links_by_feature),
        "link_value": link_value,
        "eligible": eligible,
        "eligible_by_parcel": dict(eligible_by_parcel),
        "priority_features": priority_features,
    }


def action_quality(parcel: Dict, action: Dict, action_name: str, scenario: Dict) -> float:
    marsh_term = (
        parcel["current_marsh_index"] * action["habitat_multiplier"] / scenario["sea_level_stress"]
    )
    upland_term = (
        parcel["upland_migration_index"] * action["upland_multiplier"] / scenario["development_pressure"]
    )
    salinity_term = parcel["salinity_resilience"] / scenario["salinity_shift"]
    restore_term = scenario["restoration_success"] if action_name == "restore" else 1.0
    return (marsh_term + upland_term) * salinity_term * restore_term


def feature_target(feature: Dict, scenario: Dict) -> float:
    avg_stress = (
        scenario["sea_level_stress"]
        + scenario["development_pressure"]
        + scenario["salinity_shift"]
    ) / 3.0
    target = feature["base_target_units"] * (
        1.0 + (feature["vulnerability_weight"] - 1.0) * (avg_stress - 1.0)
    )
    return max(FEAS_TOL, target)


def derive_parameters(instance: Dict) -> Dict:
    parcels = instance["parcels"]
    actions = instance["actions"]
    features = instance["features"]
    scenarios = instance["scenarios"]
    eligible = instance["eligible"]
    links_by_feature = instance["links_by_feature"]

    costs = {}
    maintenance = {}
    staff = {}
    nursery = {}
    quality = {}
    target = {}
    max_possible_cover = {}

    for pid, action_name in eligible:
        pdata = parcels[pid]
        adata = actions[action_name]
        costs[(pid, action_name)] = pdata["acquisition_cost"] * adata["fixed_cost_multiplier"]
        maintenance[(pid, action_name)] = (
            pdata["annual_maintenance_cost"] * adata["maintenance_multiplier"]
        )
        staff[(pid, action_name)] = pdata["staff_days"] * adata["staff_multiplier"]
        nursery[(pid, action_name)] = adata["nursery_units"]

    for scenario in scenarios:
        sid = scenario["scenario_id"]
        for pid, action_name in eligible:
            quality[(pid, action_name, sid)] = action_quality(
                parcels[pid], actions[action_name], action_name, scenario
            )
        for fid, fdata in features.items():
            target[(fid, sid)] = feature_target(fdata, scenario)
            upper = 0.0
            for pid, base_link in links_by_feature.get(fid, []):
                best = max(quality[(pid, a, sid)] for a in instance["eligible_by_parcel"][pid])
                upper += base_link * best
            max_possible_cover[(fid, sid)] = upper

    return {
        "capital_cost": costs,
        "maintenance_cost": maintenance,
        "staff_cost": staff,
        "nursery_cost": nursery,
        "quality": quality,
        "target": target,
        "max_possible_cover": max_possible_cover,
    }


def build_model(instance: Dict, params: Dict, time_limit: float) -> Tuple[Model, Dict, Dict, Dict]:
    config = instance["config"]
    parcels = instance["parcels"]
    actions = instance["actions"]
    features = instance["features"]
    scenarios = instance["scenarios"]
    regions = instance["regions"]
    eligible = instance["eligible"]
